Using (
A.15
) and (
A.16
), we obtain that for a reasonable ind-scheme
{\mathcal{Y}}
,
we still have an identification

\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}(\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathcal{Y}}),\operatorname{Vect})\simeq\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathcal{Y}}).

A.4.7.

We shall say that an ind-scheme
{\mathcal{Y}}
is
ind-placid
if it admits a reasonable presentations
who terms are placid schemes.

Note that using (
A.15
) we obtain that in this case
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathcal{Y}})
is compactly generated
(and hence dualizable) and
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathcal{Y}})
is also compactly generated, and we have

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathcal{Y}})\simeq(\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathcal{Y}}))^{\vee}.

A.5.
D-modules on the loop and arc groups

In this section we let
G
be a connected affine algebraic group (i.e., we are not assuming
G
to be reductive).

For the duration of this section we will unburden the notation and replace

{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{x_{0}}\rightsquigarrow{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G),\,\,{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{x_{0}}\rightsquigarrow{\mathfrak{L}}(G),\,\,{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,x_{0}}\rightsquigarrow{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}.

A.5.1.

Write

{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\simeq\underset{n}{\operatorname{lim}}\,{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n}.

This presentation exhibits
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
as a placid scheme. Thus we have:

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{!}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n})

and

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\simeq\underset{n,(-)_{*}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n})\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{*}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n}).

We have a canonical identification

(A.17)

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\simeq(\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)))^{\vee}.

A.5.2.

That said, by Sect.
A.3.9
, we have a canonical identification

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\overset{\sim}{\to}\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)),\quad\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}\mapsto\underline{k}_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}.

Using this identification, we will simply write

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)=:\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)):=\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)).

A.5.3.

The group structure on
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
makes
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
into a commutative Hopf algebra in
\operatorname{DGCat}
(using !-pullbacks),
and it makes
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
into a cocommutative Hopf algebra in
\operatorname{DGCat}
(under *-pushforwards).

The counit and unit in
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
are given by

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\overset{!\text{-fiber at }1}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{pt})=\operatorname{Vect}\text{ and }\operatorname{Vect}\overset{k\mapsto\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)),

respectively.

The unit and counit in
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
are given by

\operatorname{Vect}\overset{k\mapsto\delta_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\text{ and }\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))\overset{\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G),-)}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Vect},

respectively, where
\delta_{1}
is the *-direct image of
k\in\operatorname{D}(\operatorname{pt})
under the unit map

\operatorname{pt}\to{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
.

These two structures are obtained from one another by duality, using (
A.17
).

Remark A.5.4
.

When using the notation
\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
, we will view it either as
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))

(in the comonoidal incarnation) or
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
(in the monoidal incarnation), depending on the context.

A.5.5.

We now consider the case of
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
. First off, we claim that it is ind-placid as an ind-scheme. Indeed, write

{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}=\underset{i}{``\operatorname{colim}"}\,Y_{i},

where
Y_{i}\subset{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}
are closed
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
-invariant subschemes.

Denote by
\widetilde{Y}_{i}
the preimage of
Y_{i}
in
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
. The closed embeddings

\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}_{j},

being obtained by base change from
Y_{i}\to Y_{j}
, are automatically of finite presentation.

We claim that each
\widetilde{Y}_{i}
is placid. Indeed, we can write it as

(A.18)

\underset{n}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n},

and/or

(A.19)

\underset{n}{\operatorname{lim}}\,K_{n}\backslash\widetilde{Y}_{i}.

Note that these two inverse families are automatically equivalent: for every
n
there exists
n^{\prime}
such that the projection

\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}

factors as

(A.20)

\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to K_{n^{\prime}}\backslash\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}

and vice versa.

A.5.6.

Hence, we obtain that the categories

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\text{ and }\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))

are compactly generated and dual to each other.

Explicitly,

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\underset{i,(-)^{!}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{D}^{!}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})\simeq\underset{i,(-)_{*}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}^{!}(\widetilde{Y}_{i}),

while for every
i
,

\operatorname{D}^{!}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{!}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}(\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}),

and

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\underset{i,(-)^{!}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{D}_{*}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})\simeq\underset{i,(-)_{*}!}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}_{*}(\widetilde{Y}_{i}),

while for every
i
,

\operatorname{D}_{*}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})\simeq\underset{n,(-)_{*}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{D}(\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n})\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{*}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}(\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}).

In the above presentations of
\operatorname{D}^{!}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})
and
\operatorname{D}_{*}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})
, one can replace the family
\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}
by

K_{n}\backslash\widetilde{Y}_{i}
.

A.5.7.

Note also that we have

(A.21)

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{!}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n})

and

(A.22)

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\underset{n,(-)_{*}}{\operatorname{lim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n})\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{*}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n}),

and we can also replace the family

n\mapsto{\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n}

by

n\mapsto K_{n}\backslash{\mathfrak{L}}(G).

A.5.8.

Let

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\in\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))

be the object equal to the image of

\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n}}[-2\dim({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n})]\in\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n})

for some/any
n
under the presentation

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{*}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/K_{n}).

It is clear that
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
is indeed a fake dualizing sheaf, i.e., it gives rise
to an equivalence

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\to\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)),\quad{\mathcal{F}}\mapsto{\mathcal{F}}\overset{!}{\otimes}\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

We define

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\in\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))

similarly, using the presentation

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\underset{n,(-)^{*}}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\operatorname{D}(K_{n}\backslash{\mathfrak{L}}(G)).

A.5.9.

Let
\mu:G\to{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
be the modular character (i.e., the determinant of the adjoint action). Let

\deg(\mu)
be the corresponding function

\pi_{0}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\overset{\mu}{\to}\pi_{0}({\mathfrak{L}}({\mathbb{G}}_{m}))\simeq{\mathbb{Z}}.

Proposition A.5.10
.

The objects
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
and
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}[2\deg(\mu)]
are canonically isomorphic.

Proof.

For every
i
, let

(A.23)

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{\widetilde{Y}_{i}}\text{ and }\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{\widetilde{Y}_{i}}

be the objects of
\operatorname{D}_{*}(\widetilde{Y}_{i})
defined by a similar procedure, using the presentation of
\widetilde{Y}_{i}

as (
A.18
) and (
A.19
), respectively.

It suffices to exhibit a compatible family of isomorphisms

(A.24)

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{\widetilde{Y}_{i}}\simeq\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{\widetilde{Y}_{i}}[2\deg(\mu)].

Note that the objects (
A.23
) are associated to the two dimension theories on
\widetilde{Y}_{i}
: one
attaches to

\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}

the integer
d_{n}:=\dim({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n})
, and another attaches the same integer to

\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to K_{n}\backslash\widetilde{Y}_{i}.

It suffices to show that these two dimension theories are in fact equivalent up to the shift by
\deg(\mu)
.

To do this, fix an integer
n
and let
n^{\prime}
be as in (
A.20
). The required equality follows from the fact that the resulting map

K_{n^{\prime}}\backslash\widetilde{Y}_{i}\to\widetilde{Y}_{i}/K_{n}

is smooth of relative dimension
\dim(K_{n}/K_{n^{\prime}})+\deg(\mu)
.

∎

A.5.11.

Assume that
G
is unimodular. In this case, using the identification of Proposition
A.5.10
, we will use the notation

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}=:\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}:=\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

Thus,
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
gives rise to a canonical identification

(A.25)

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\operatorname{D}^{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)).

We will use the notation:

\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))=:\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)):=\operatorname{D}^{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)).

A.5.12.

The structure of group-object in ind-schemes defines on
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
a structure of commutative Hopf algebra,
and on
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
a structure of cocommutative Hopf algebra.

These two structures are obtained from one another
by duality.

Remark A.5.13
.

When using the notation
\operatorname{D}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
, we will view it either as
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))

(in the comonoidal incarnation) or
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
(in the monoidal incarnation), depending on the context.

Appendix B
Categorical representations of (loop) groups

In this section we (re)collect some facts pertaining to the notion of action of a group on a category.
We start with groups of finite type, and then develop the theory for the arc and loop groups.

B.1.
The case of groups of finite type

In this subsection, we let
H
be an affine algebraic group (of finite type).

B.1.1.

We consider
\operatorname{D}(H)
as a monoidal category in
\operatorname{DGCat}
under convolution. We set

H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}:=\operatorname{D}(H){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}.

Note that Verdier duality identifies
\operatorname{D}(H)
with its own dual. This allows us to view
\operatorname{D}(H)

as comonoidal category via

\operatorname{D}(H)\overset{\operatorname{mult}^{!}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{D}(H\times H)\simeq\operatorname{D}(H)\otimes\operatorname{D}(H).

We can tautologically interpret
H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
as
\operatorname{D}(H){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{comod}}}
for this structure.

B.1.2.

Push-forward (resp., pullback) with respect to the diagonal map extends the above monoidal (resp., comonoidal)
structure on
\operatorname{DGCat}
to a structure of cocommutative (resp., commutative) Hopf algebra object in
\operatorname{DGCat}
.
This structure endows

H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
with a symmetric monoidal structure, compatible with the forgetful functor

(B.1)

H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}.

The unit of this symmetric monoidal structure is a copy of
\operatorname{Vect}
, equipped with the trivial action of
H
, i.e. the coaction map

\operatorname{Vect}\to\operatorname{D}(H)\otimes\operatorname{Vect}\simeq\operatorname{D}(H)

is the functor
k\mapsto\omega_{H}
.

The corresponding action map

\operatorname{D}(H)\simeq\operatorname{D}(H)\otimes\operatorname{Vect}\to\operatorname{Vect}

is the functor of de Rham cochains, denoted
\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}(H)
.

B.1.3.

As is the case for modules over Hopf algebras, an object of
H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
is dualizable if and only if its
image under (
B.1
) is dualizable, and the functor (
B.1
), being
symmetric monoidal, commutes with duality.

B.1.4.

Let
F:{\mathbf{C}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
be 1-morphism in
H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, and suppose that
F
admits a left (resp., right) adjoint,
when viewed as a 1-morphism in
\operatorname{DGCat}
. We claim that this adjoint then exists in
H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
.

Indeed, a priori
F^{L}
(resp.,
F^{R}
) will be op-lax (resp., lax) compatible with the action of
\operatorname{D}(H)
.
However, it is easy to see that the fact that
H
is is a group (as opposed to a monoid) forces any
op-lax (resp., lax) compatible functor between module categories to be strictly compatible.

Indeed, for a point
S
-point
h
of
H
and a lax-compatible functor
F:{\mathbf{C}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
, the morphism

F\circ h\overset{\alpha_{h}}{\to}h\circ F

(viewed as a natural transformation beween functors
{\mathbf{C}}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{D}(S)\rightrightarrows{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\otimes\operatorname{D}(S)
)
admits an inverse given by

h\circ F\simeq h\circ F\circ h^{-1}\circ h\overset{\alpha_{h^{-1}}}{\to}h\circ h^{-1}\circ F\circ h\simeq F\circ h,

and similarly for op-lax functors.

B.1.5.

Note that the Verdier duality identification

(B.2)

\operatorname{D}(H)^{\vee}\simeq\operatorname{D}(H)

is compatible with the structure of object of
(H\times H){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
on the two sides.

In particular, the left and right adjoints of the forgetful functor (
B.1
) are canonically isomorphic.

Remark B.1.6
.

Note that we can also interpret (
B.2
) as follows: the functor

\operatorname{D}(H)\otimes\operatorname{D}(H)\overset{\text{convolution}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{D}(H)\overset{\text{counit}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Vect}

is a duality pairing, which differs from the Verdier one by the inversion operation on
H
.

This pairing makes
\operatorname{D}(H)
into a Frobenius algebra in
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

B.1.7.

For
{\mathbf{C}}\in H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, set

{\mathbf{C}}^{H}:=\operatorname{Funct}_{H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}}(\operatorname{Vect},{\mathbf{C}}).

We have a pair of adjoint functors

{\operatorname{oblv}}_{H}:{\mathbf{C}}^{H}\rightleftarrows{\mathbf{C}}:\operatorname{Av}^{H}_{*}.

B.1.8.

Consider the category
\operatorname{Vect}
. The forgetful functor

{\operatorname{oblv}}_{H}:\operatorname{Vect}^{H}\to\operatorname{Vect}

is comonadic with the comonad in question is given by tensor product with
\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}(H)
, on
which the coalgebra structure is induced by the group structure on
H
.

Equivalently,

\operatorname{Vect}^{H}\simeq\operatorname{C}_{\cdot}(H){\operatorname{-mod}},

where
\operatorname{C}_{\cdot}(H)
is an algebra via the group structure on
H
.

The category
\operatorname{Vect}^{H}
carries a natural (symmetric) monoidal structure. It corresponds to the structure
on
\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}(H)
(resp.,
\operatorname{C}_{\cdot}(H)
) of commutative (resp., cocommutative) Hopf algebra.

B.1.9.

For any
{\mathbf{C}}
, the category
{\mathbf{C}}^{H}
is naturally tensored over
\operatorname{Vect}^{H}
. The comonad

{\operatorname{oblv}}_{H}\circ\operatorname{Av}^{H}_{*}

on
{\mathbf{C}}^{H}
is given by tensoring with the coalgebra object

\operatorname{Av}^{H}_{*}(k)\in\operatorname{Vect}^{H}.

Note that the underlying object of
\operatorname{Vect}
, i.e.,
{\operatorname{oblv}}_{H}\circ\operatorname{Av}^{H}_{*}(k)
, identifies with
\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}(H)
.

In particular, this implies that the functor
{\operatorname{oblv}}_{H}:{\mathbf{C}}^{H}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is fully faithful if
H
is unipotent.

B.1.10.

For
{\mathbf{C}}\in H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, let
{\mathbf{C}}_{H}\in\operatorname{DGCat}
be the object such that

\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}}_{H},{\mathbf{C}}_{0})=\operatorname{Funct}_{H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}_{0}),\quad{\mathbf{C}}_{0}\in\operatorname{DGCat},

where
H
acts trivially on
{\mathbf{C}}_{0}
.

Note that the functor

\operatorname{Av}^{H}_{*}:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{H}

is
H
-invariant, and hence gives rise to a functor

(B.3)

{\mathbf{C}}_{H}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{H}.

B.1.11.

We have the following basic assertion, see, e.g.,
[
Ga4
, Theorem B.1.2]
:

Proposition B.1.12
.

The functor (
B.3
) is an equivalence.

Corollary B.1.13
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}\in H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
be dualizable as a DG category. Then
{\mathbf{C}}^{H}
is also dualizable and
we have a canonical equivalence

({\mathbf{C}}^{H})^{\vee}\simeq({\mathbf{C}}^{\vee})^{H}.

Corollary B.1.14
.

For
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\in H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, we have a canonical equivalence

{\mathbf{C}}_{1}\underset{\operatorname{D}(H)}{\otimes}{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\simeq({\mathbf{C}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{2})^{H}.

Corollary B.1.15
.

The functor

{\mathbf{C}}\mapsto{\mathbf{C}}^{H},\quad H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

commutes with colimits and tensor products by objects of
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

B.2.
The case of arc groups

In this and the next subsections we keep the notational change from Sect.
A.5
.

B.2.1.

We set

{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}:=\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{comod}}}\simeq\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}.

The entire discussion in Sect.
B.1
is applicable in this case. Moreover, as we shall
see below, the study of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
reduces to that of
H{\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
for finite-dimensional
quotients
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\rightrightarrows H
.

B.2.2.

For
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
and an integer
n
, let
{\mathbf{e}}_{n}
be the endofunctor of
{\mathbf{C}}

equal to
{\operatorname{oblv}}_{K_{n}}\circ\operatorname{Av}^{K_{n}}_{*}
.

Note that
{\mathbf{e}}_{n}
can be thought of as the action of

\underline{k}_{K_{n}}\in\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)).

The essential image of
{\mathbf{e}}_{n}
is the
full subcategory

{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}.

Since
K_{n}
is normal in
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
, the category
{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}}
is stable under the
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
-action, i.e., forms
a subobject in
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
. Moreover, the action of
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
on
{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}}
factors through

{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)/K_{n}
.

B.2.3.

Consider the colimit

(B.4)

\underset{n}{\operatorname{colim}}\,{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}},

formed in
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, where the transition functors are the natural inclusions.

Since the index family (i.e.,
{\mathbf{Z}}^{\geq 0}
) is filtered, the underlying DG category of (
B.4
)
is a similar colimit taken in
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

Note also that since for
n_{1}\leq n_{2}
, the embedding

{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n_{1}}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n_{2}}}

admits a right adjoint, we can rewrite (
B.4
) also as

(B.5)

\underset{n}{\operatorname{lim}}\,{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}},

where the limit is taken with respect to the above averaging functors.

B.2.4.

We have a tautologically defined map in
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}

(B.6)

\underset{n}{\operatorname{colim}}\,{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}}\to{\mathbf{C}}.

We claim:

Proposition B.2.5
.

The map (
B.6
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since the forgetful functor

{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

is conservative, it suffices to show that the functor (
B.6
) is an equivalence
of DG categories.

Since the category of indices is filtered, and each
{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
is fully faithful
admitting a colimit-preserving right adjoint, it follows automatically that the functor
(
B.6
) is fully faithful.

Hence, it remains to show that it is essentially surjective. I.e., it suffices to exhibit any object
of
{\mathbf{C}}
as a colimit of objects belonging to
{\mathbf{C}}^{K_{n}}
for some
n
. In fact, we claim that
for any
{\mathbf{c}}\in{\mathbf{C}}
, the naturally defined map

(B.7)

\underset{n}{\operatorname{colim}}\,{\mathbf{e}}_{n}({\mathbf{c}})\to{\mathbf{C}}

is an isomorphism.

Indeed, (
B.7
) follows from the isomorphism

\underset{n}{\operatorname{colim}}\,\underline{k}_{K_{n}}\simeq\delta_{1}

in
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
.

∎

B.3.
The case of loop groups

We finally consider the case of
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
, and define what we mean by categories acted on by it.

B.3.1.

We define

{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}:=\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{comod}}}=\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}.

Remarks as in Sect.
B.1.2
-
B.1.4
apply
equally well to
{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
.

B.3.2.

For
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, set

{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}:=\operatorname{Funct}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}}(\operatorname{Vect},{\mathbf{C}}).

In particular, we can consider the (symmetric monoidal) category
\operatorname{Vect}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
.

B.3.3.

We claim:

Lemma-Construction B.3.4
.

There is a canonical equivalence of symmetric monoidal categories

\operatorname{Vect}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\simeq\operatorname{C}_{\cdot}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)){\operatorname{-mod}},

compatible with the forgeftul functors to
\operatorname{Vect}
.

Proof.

For
V\in\operatorname{Vect}
, its lift to an object of
\operatorname{Vect}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
is a datum of structure of an object of

\operatorname{Tot}(\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)^{\bullet}))

with terms
\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)^{\bullet}}\otimes V
, where
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)^{\bullet}
is the simplicial ind-scheme
constructed out of
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
, viewed as a group-object in ind-schemes.

Such a datum amounts to an isomorphism

(B.8)

\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\otimes V\simeq\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\otimes V,

in
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
, satisfying the natural associativity conditions.

The map
\to
in (
B.8
) gives rise by adjunction to a map

(B.9)

\operatorname{C}_{\cdot}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\otimes V=\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}_{c}({\mathfrak{L}}(G),\omega_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\otimes V\to V,

and the associativity datum on (
B.8
) is equivalent to the associativity datum for
(
B.9
).

Vice versa, starting from (
B.9
) we construct a map
\to
(
B.8
),
and the associativity for (
B.9
) implies that (
B.8
) is automtically
an isomorphism.

∎

B.3.5.

Consider
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
as an object of
({\mathfrak{L}}(G)\times{\mathfrak{L}}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
. This can be either done by viewing

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
as a bimodule over itself. Or, equivalently, we can view
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
as the dual
(inside
({\mathfrak{L}}(G)\times{\mathfrak{L}}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
) of
\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
, viewed as a bi-comodule over itself.

Let us now assume that
G
is unimodular. Recall the object

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\in\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)),

see Sect.
A.5.11
.

Its interpretation as
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
implies that it is naturally an object of

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)-L},

where the superscript “L” refers to the left action of
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
on
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
.

Similarly, its interpretation as
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
implies that it is naturally an object of

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)-R}.

However, we claim:

Lemma B.3.6
.

The object
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
naturally lifts to an object of

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\times{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

Proof.

We have to show that for points
g_{1},g_{2}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
, the diagram

(B.10)

\begin{CD}(g_{1}\cdot\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\cdot g_{2}@>{\sim}>{}>g_{1}\cdot(\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\cdot g_{2})\\
@V{\sim}V{}V@V{}V{\sim}V\\
(g_{1}\cdot\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\cdot g_{2}&&g_{1}\cdot(\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\cdot g_{2})\\
@V{\sim}V{}V@V{}V{\sim}V\\
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\cdot g_{2}&&g_{1}\cdot\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\\
@V{\sim}V{}V@V{}V{\sim}V\\
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\cdot g_{2}&&g_{1}\cdot\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\\
@V{\sim}V{}V@V{}V{\sim}V\\
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,R}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}@>{}>{}>\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\end{CD}

commutes, along with the higher compatibilities.

First, we claim that the higher compatibilities hold automatically, since

{\mathop{\operatorname{{\mathcal{H}}\it om}}}(\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)},\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*,L}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\simeq\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))

is coconnective.

Hence, we only need to check the commutation of (
B.10
) up to homotopy. However, the latter
follows immediately from the construction of the isomorphism of Proposition
A.5.10
.

∎

As a corollary, we obtain:

Corollary B.3.7
.

The isomorphism (
A.25
) lifts to an identification of objects in
({\mathfrak{L}}(G)\times{\mathfrak{L}}(G)){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
.

Corollary B.3.8
.

The left and right adjoints to the forgetful functor

{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

are canonically isomorphic.

Remark B.3.9
.

Remark
B.1.6
applies here as well for the pairing

\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\otimes\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\overset{\text{convolution}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\simeq\operatorname{D}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\overset{\operatorname{counit}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Vect}.

This pairing makes
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
into a Frobenius algebra in
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

B.4.
The case of a reductive
G

Up to now, the discussion of
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
was valid for
any
affine algebraic group
G
. We will now assume that

G
is reductive, and exploit some special features of this case. The key to what we are about to say is that for
G

reductive, the affine Grassmannian

{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}:={\mathfrak{L}}(G)/{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)

is ind-proper.

B.4.1.

We claim:

Proposition B.4.2
.

The forgetful functor

{\operatorname{oblv}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}:{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}

admits a left adjoint, to be denoted
\operatorname{Av}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}_{!}
.

Proof.

Note that we can interpret
{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}
as

(\operatorname{D}({\operatorname{Gr}}_{G})\otimes{\mathbf{C}})^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

Hence, it suffices to show that the functor

\operatorname{Vect}\to\operatorname{D}({\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}),\quad k\mapsto\omega_{{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}}

admits a left adjoint in
{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, or equivalently, in
\operatorname{Vect}
.

However, the left adjoint in question is provided by
\operatorname{C}^{\cdot}({\operatorname{Gr}}_{G},-)
.

∎

B.4.3.

Set

\operatorname{Sph}_{G}:=\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\times{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}.

The usual Hecke algebra construction shows that the monoidal structure on
\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
induces
one on
\operatorname{Sph}_{G}
. Moreover, for every
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, we have a canonical action of
\operatorname{Sph}_{G}
on

{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}G}
.

B.4.4.

Note that we can consider
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
as an associative algebra object of
\operatorname{Sph}_{G}
;
when viewed as such, let us denote it by
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}
.

Unwinding, we obtain that the monad

(B.11)

{\operatorname{oblv}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}\circ\operatorname{Av}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}_{!}

acting on
{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}
is given by the action of
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}
.

Therefore, by the Barr-Beck-Lurie theorem, we can identify

{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\simeq\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}{\operatorname{-mod}}({\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}).

B.4.5.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be an object
{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
. Let
{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
be the object of
\operatorname{DGCat}
such that

\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)},{\mathbf{C}}_{0})\simeq\operatorname{Funct}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}_{0}),\quad{\mathbf{C}}_{0}\in\operatorname{DGCat},

where in the right-hand side,
{\mathbf{C}}_{0}
is considered as equipped with the trivial
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
-action.

B.4.6.

For
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{0}\in\operatorname{DGCat}
, let us view
\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}_{0})

as an object of
{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
via the action on the source.

By Proposition
B.3
(applied to
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
), we have

(B.12)

\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}_{0})^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)}\simeq\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)},{\mathbf{C}}_{0}).

Unwinding, we obtain that the action of
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}
on the left-hand side
corresponds to the action of
\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}
on the source in the right-hand side.

B.4.7.

Take
{\mathbf{C}}_{0}={\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
, and consider the object of the category
\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)},{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})

given by
\operatorname{Av}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}_{!}
. Since the functor
\operatorname{Av}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}_{!}
is acted on by
the monad (
B.11
), we obtain that the above object lifts to an object of

\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}{\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)},{\mathbf{C}}_{0}))\simeq\\
\simeq\omega^{\operatorname{fake},*}_{\operatorname{Hecke}}{\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}_{0})^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)})\simeq\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{DGCat}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}_{0})^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

Thus, the functor
\operatorname{Av}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)\to{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}_{!}
may be viewed as a point in

\operatorname{Funct}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}}({\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})\simeq\operatorname{Funct}({\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)},{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}).

B.4.8.

We have (see
[
Ga4
, Theorem D.1.4(b)]
):

Proposition B.4.9
.

The above functor

{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

is an equivalence.

B.4.10.

From Proposition
B.4.9
we obtain:

Corollary B.4.11
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
be dualizable as a category. Then
{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}
is also dualizable and
we have a canonical equivalence

({\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)})^{\vee}\simeq({\mathbf{C}}^{\vee})^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

Corollary B.4.12
.

For
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\in{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}
, we have a canonical isomorphism

{\mathbf{C}}_{1}\underset{\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))}{\otimes}{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\simeq({\mathbf{C}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{2})^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}.

Corollary B.4.13
.

The functor

{\mathbf{C}}\mapsto{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)},\quad{\mathfrak{L}}(G){\operatorname{-}\!{\mathbf{mod}}}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

commutes with colimits and tensor products by objects of
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

Remark B.4.14
.

One can rewrite the natural transformation in Proposition
B.4.9
also as follows.

Let
G
be an arbitrary unimodular group. As is the case for an arbitrary augmented algebra
A
,
which is isomorphic to its own dual as a bimodule, there is a canonical natural transformation

(B.13)

M_{A}\to M^{A},\quad M\in A{\operatorname{-mod}}.

Unwinding, one can see that (
B.14
) is the map (
B.14
) for

A:=\operatorname{D}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\in\operatorname{DGCat}
.

Note, however, that the resulting natural transformation

(B.14)

{\mathbf{C}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}(G)}

will
not
in general be an isomorphism, unless
G
is reductive.

For example, if
G=N
is unipotent and
{\mathbf{C}}=\operatorname{Vect}
, the natural transformation (
B.14
)
will be zero.

Appendix C
Factorization categories and modules

Most of the material in this appendix, except Sect.
C.11
- Sect.
C.13
, follows
[
GLC2
, Sect. C]
. The reader is referred to
loc.cit.
for more details.

C.1.
Unital factorization spaces

C.1.1.

A
categorical prestack
is a functor

(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\operatorname{Cat}.

Let

\operatorname{CatPreStk}:=\operatorname{Fun}((\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Cat})

be the
2-category
of categorical prestacks.

C.1.2.

Given a categorical prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
, its value at an affine scheme
S
is denoted by
{\mathcal{Y}}(S)
. By Yoneda’s lemma, objects in
{\mathcal{Y}}(S)
are identified with morphisms
S\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, while morphisms in
{\mathcal{Y}}(S)
can be identified with 2-morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

in
\operatorname{CatPreStk}
.

C.1.3.

The
unital Ran space
is the categorical prestack
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
that attaches to an affine test scheme
S
the
category
of finite subsets of
\operatorname{Hom}(S,X)
, where morphisms are given by inclusions of subsets.

In particular, a
k
-point
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is just a finite subset of closed points of
X
.

C.1.4.
Remark

We warn the readers that we do not require
\underline{x}
to be nonempty. In particular, there is a canonical point
\emptyset\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
corresponding to the empty subset. See Sect.
C.13
for the reason we make this choice.

Note that
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is denoted
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl},*}
in
[
GLC2
, Sect. C.5]
.

C.1.5.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
be a categorical prestack. A
coCartesian space over
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a categorical prestack
{\mathcal{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
such that for any affine scheme
S
, the functor
{\mathcal{Z}}(S)\to{\mathcal{Y}}(S)
is a left fibration, i.e., a coCartesian fibration in groupoids.

Dually, we define the notion of
Cartesian spaces over
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

C.1.6.
Remark

Roughly speaking, a coCartesian space
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is an assignment as follows.

•

For any point
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, a (usual) prestack
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}
;

•

For any
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
, a morphism
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}

that is compatible with compositions.

The above data should depend “algebraically” on
\underline{x}
and
\underline{x}^{\prime}
.

C.1.7.
Example

We have a coCartesian space
{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
such that
{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,\underline{x}}
is as in Sect.
1.2.4
, while the morphism

{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,\underline{x}}\to{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,\underline{x}^{\prime}}\;

(for
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
) sends
({\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{\operatorname{glob}},\beta)
to
({\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{\operatorname{glob}},\beta|_{X\setminus\underline{x}^{\prime}})
.

C.1.8.
Example

We have a Cartesian space
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
such that
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{\underline{x}}:=G(\mathcal{D}_{\underline{x}})
, while the morphism

{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}\to{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{\underline{x}}

(for
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
) is induced by restriction along
\mathcal{D}_{\underline{x}}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}
.

C.1.9.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
be a categorical prestack. There is a common generalization of coCartesian spaces and Cartesian spaces over
{\mathcal{Y}}
. A
corr-space over
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a categorical prestack
{\mathcal{Z}}\to{\mathcal{Y}}
such that for any affine scheme
S
, the functor
{\mathcal{Z}}(S)\to{\mathcal{Y}}(S)
is a
fibration in correspondences
(see
[
GLC2
, C.10]
).

C.1.10.
Remark

Roughly speaking, a corr-space
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is an assignment as follows.

•

For any point
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, a (usual) prestack
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}
;

•

For any
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
, a correspondence
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}
or prestacks that is compatible with compositions.

In particular, we have

{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}\simeq{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}}

and

{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}\times_{{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}}\simeq{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime\prime}}.

The corr-space
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
is coCartesian (resp. Cartesian) over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
when the morphisms
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}
) are invertible.

C.1.11.
Example

We have a corr-space
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
such that
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{\underline{x}}:=G(\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{D}}_{\underline{x}})
,
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}:=G(\mathcal{D}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}\setminus\underline{x})
, while the correspondence

{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{\underline{x}}\leftarrow{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}\to{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}

(for
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
) is induced by restriction along

\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{D}}_{\underline{x}}\to\mathcal{D}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}\setminus\underline{x}\leftarrow\overset{\circ}{\mathcal{D}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}.

C.1.12.

Note that
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is an abelian monoid object in
\operatorname{CatPreStk}
, with the addition map given by

\operatorname{union}:{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\times{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},\;(\underline{x},\underline{y})\mapsto\underline{x}\cup\underline{y}.

For any finite set
I
, we obtain a map

\operatorname{union}_{I}:\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}.

Note that when
I=\emptyset
, this is the map
\emptyset:\operatorname{pt}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
.

C.1.13.

As in the non-unital case, we consider the disjoint locus

({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\times{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})_{\operatorname{disj}}\subseteq{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\times{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},

which sends an affine scheme
S
to the category

\{(\underline{x},\underline{y})\in({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\times{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})(S)\;|\;\operatorname{Graph}_{\underline{x}}\cap\operatorname{Graph}_{\underline{y}}=\emptyset\}.

Similarly, for any finite set
I
, we can define

\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}}\subseteq\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}.

C.1.14.

Now a
unital
(resp.
counital
,
corr-unital
) factorization space
{\mathcal{T}}
is a
coCartesian space
(resp.
Cartesian space
,
corr-space
)
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
equipped with a
multiplicative structure over the disjoint loci
. In other words, for any finite set
I
, we have an isomorphism

\operatorname{union_{I}}^{-1}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}})|_{\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}}}\simeq(\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}})|_{\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}}}

and a homotopy-coherent datum of associativity and commutativity. Here
\operatorname{union}_{I}^{-1}
is the change-of-base along the map
\operatorname{union}_{I}
.

C.1.15.
Remark

Roughly speaking, a corr-unital factorization space
{\mathcal{T}}
consists of the following data

•

Prestacks
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}
and correspondences
{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}
as in Sect.
C.1.10
;

•

For any finite collection
\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}
(
i\in I
) such that
\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}\cap\underline{x}_{j}^{\prime}=\emptyset
(
i\neq j
), ca ommutative diagram

\textstyle{\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{T}}_{\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}}

that is compatible with compositions.

Such a
{\mathcal{T}}
is unital (resp. counital) means when the leftward (resp. rightward) morphisms are invertible.

C.1.16.
Example

The corr-space
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
has a natural corr-unital factorization structure. We denote the resulting corr-unital factorization space by
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
.

Similarly, we have a unital factorization space
{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G}
and a counital factorization space
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)
.

C.2.
Crystal of categories over the unital Ran space

C.2.1.

Given a categorical prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
, a
crystal of categories

\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
over
{\mathcal{Y}}
is an assignment as follows.

•

For an affine scheme
S
and a morphism
y:S\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, assign a
\operatorname{D}(S)
-module category
{\mathbf{C}}_{y}
;

•

For affine schemes
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
and a 2-morphism

(C.1)

\textstyle{S_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{S_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{Y}},}

(i.e. a morphism
\alpha:y_{1}\circ f\to y_{2}
in
{\mathcal{Y}}(S_{2})
), assign a functor

{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbf{C}}_{y_{1}}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{y_{2}}

intertwining the action of
f^{!}:\operatorname{D}(S_{1})\to\operatorname{D}(S_{2})
, such that the induced functor

{\mathbf{C}}_{y_{1}}\otimes_{\operatorname{D}(S_{1})}\operatorname{D}(S_{2})\to{\mathbf{C}}_{y_{2}}

is an equivalence when
\alpha
is invertible.

•

A homotopy-coherent system of compatibilities for compositions.

C.2.2.
Remark

Let

\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}

be the coCartesian fibration corresponding to the functor
{\mathcal{Y}}
. Let

\widetilde{\mathbf{CrysCat}}\to(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}

be the coCartesian fibration of 2-categories corresponding to the functor
S\mapsto\operatorname{D}(S)\operatorname{-mod}
. Then a crystal of categories
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
over
{\mathcal{Y}}
is
defined
to be a
(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}
-functor
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{CrysCat}}
preserving coCartesian arrows.

C.2.3.
Example

We have the
constant crystal of categories

\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}})
over
{\mathcal{Y}}
which assignes to
y\in{\mathcal{Y}}(S)
the cateogry
\operatorname{D}(S)
and assignes to a 2-morphism
\alpha
the functor
f^{!}:\operatorname{D}(S_{1})\to\operatorname{D}(S_{2})
.

C.2.4.
Remark

Roughly speaking, a crystal of categories
{\mathbf{A}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is an assignment as follows.

•

For any point
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, assign a DG category
{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}
;

•

For any inclusion
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
, assign a functor

\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}:{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}\to{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}

that is compatible with compositions.

The above data should depend “algebraically” on
\underline{x}
and
\underline{x^{\prime}}
. This means we also allow
\underline{x}
to be affine points
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}(S)
, and the above data should be contravariantly functorial in
S
.

C.2.5.

Given a corr-space
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, under some mild finiteness assumptions, we can construct a crystal of categories
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}})
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
such that

•

For any
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
,

\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}})_{\underline{x}}\simeq\operatorname{D}({\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}})

•

For any inclusion
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
, the functor

\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}:\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}})_{\underline{x}}\to\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{T}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}})_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}

is given by
!
-pull-
*
-push along the correspondence

{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}}\leftarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}.

Here the finiteness assumptions are required such that:

•

The category
\operatorname{D}({\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}})
is well-defined
25
25

25

In fact, we need to consider all finite type affine points
\underline{x}:S\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
.
;

•

The
!
-pullback functor
\operatorname{D}({\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}})\to\operatorname{D}({\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}})
is well-defined, and the
*
-pushforward functor
\operatorname{D}({\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{D}({\mathcal{T}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}})
is well-defined.

•

The above
!
-pullback functors and
*
-pushforward functors have base-change isomorphisms.

C.2.6.
Example

The coCartesian space
{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
produces a crystal of categories
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\operatorname{Gr}}_{G})
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, due to the fact that each
{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,\underline{x}}
is ind-finite type.

The Cartesian space
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
produces crystals of categories
\underline{\operatorname{D}}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
and
\underline{\operatorname{D}}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
, due to the fact that each
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{\underline{x}}
is placid (see Sect.
A.3.7
). Note however that we can write

\underline{\operatorname{D}}^{!}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))=:\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)):=\underline{\operatorname{D}}_{*}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))

because of Sect.
A.5.2
.

Similarly, the corr-space
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
produces a crystal of categories
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
.

C.3.
Morphisms between crystals of categories

There are two notions of morphisms between crystals of categories over a categorical prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
:
lax functors
and
strict functors
.

C.3.1.

A
lax functor

F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is an assignment as follows.

•

For any
y:S\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, assign a
\operatorname{D}(S)
-linear functor
F_{y}:{\mathbf{C}}_{y}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{y}^{\prime}
;

•

For any 2-morphism (
C.1
), assign a
\operatorname{D}(S)
-linear natural transformation

\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}_{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{F_{y}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}_{y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{y^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{F_{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}_{y^{\prime}},}

such that it is invertible if
\alpha
is so.

•

A homotopy-coherent system of compatibilities for compositions.

A lax functor
F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is
strict
if the above natural transformations are all invertible.

The totality of crystals of categories over
{\mathcal{Y}}
and lax functors gives a 2-category
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})
. There is a 1-full subcategory

\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{strict}}({\mathcal{Y}})\subseteq\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})

with morphisms being strict functors.

We equip
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})
with the natural symmetric monoidal structure given by the formula

({\mathbf{C}}\otimes{\mathbf{D}})_{y}:={\mathbf{C}}_{y}\otimes_{\operatorname{D}(S)}{\mathbf{D}}_{y},\;(y:S\to Y).

C.3.2.
Remark

Following Sect.
C.2.2
, a lax functor
F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is
defined
to be a (right-)lax natural transformation (over
(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}
) between the corresponding functors

{\mathbf{C}},{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}:\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to\widetilde{\mathbf{CrysCat}}

such that its value at any coCartesian arrow in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}
is strict, while a strict functor
F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
is just a strict natural transformation.

A 2-morphism in
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})
is
defined
to be a (strict)
modification
26
26

26

There is no room for laxness for modifications because 3-morphisms in
\widetilde{\mathbf{CrysCat}}
are invertible.
between such lax natural transformations.
In other words, they are 2-morphisms in
\operatorname{Fun}_{(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}}(\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}},\widetilde{\mathbf{CrysCat}})
.

C.3.3.
Example

A lax functor
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}})\to{\mathbf{C}}
is called a
lax global section
of
{\mathbf{C}}
. It is an assignment as follows.

•

For any
y:S\to{\mathcal{Y}}
, assign an object
{\mathcal{F}}_{y}\in{\mathbf{C}}_{y}
;

•

For any 2-morphism (
C.1
), assign a morphism
{\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha}:{\mathbf{C}}_{\alpha}({\mathcal{F}}_{y})\to{\mathcal{F}}_{y^{\prime}}
in
{\mathbf{C}}_{y^{\prime}}
such that it is invertible if
\alpha
is so.

•

A homotopy-coherent system of compatibilities for compositions.

The collection of lax global sections of
{\mathbf{C}}
form a DG category
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\operatorname{lax}}
, which is denoted
\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}},{\mathbf{C}})
in
[
GLC2
, Sect. C.2]
.

Similarly, a strict functor
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}})\to{\mathbf{C}}
is called a
strict global section
of
{\mathbf{C}}
. It is an assignment as above such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{\alpha}
is
always
invertible. The collection of strict global sections of
{\mathbf{C}}
form a full subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\operatorname{strict}}\subseteq{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\operatorname{lax}}
, which is denoted by
\Gamma^{\operatorname{strict}}({\mathcal{Y}},{\mathbf{C}})
in
loc.cit.
.

C.3.4.
Example

Objects in

\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\operatorname{lax}}\simeq\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}},\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}}))

are called
lax D-modules
on
{\mathcal{Y}}
, while those in

\operatorname{D}^{\operatorname{strict}}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}})_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\operatorname{strict}}\simeq\Gamma^{\operatorname{strict}}({\mathcal{Y}},\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}}))

are
strict D-modules
on
{\mathcal{Y}}
.

C.3.5.
Remark

Following Sect.
C.2.4
, roughly speaking, a lax (resp. strict) functor
F:{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
consists of the following data:

•

For any point
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, assign a functor
F_{\underline{x}}:{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}\to{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}^{\prime}
;

•

For any inclusion
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
, assign a natural transformation (resp. isomorphism)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{F_{\underline{x}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}_{\underline{x}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{F_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}},}

that is compatible with compositions.

In particular, a lax (resp. strict) global section
{\mathcal{A}}
of
{\mathbf{A}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
consists of the following data:

•

For any point
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, assign an object
{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}}\in{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}
;

•

For any inclusion
\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}
, assign a morphism (resp. isomorphism)

{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}:\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}})\to{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}

that is compatible with compositions.

C.3.6.

Given a morphism
f:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
between categorical prestacks, there is a symmetric monoidal functor

f^{*}:\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Z}})\to\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})

given by the formula

(f^{*}{\mathbf{C}})_{y}\simeq{\mathbf{C}}_{f(y)},

where
y:S\to{\mathcal{Y}}
is an affine point of
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
f(y)=f\circ y
is an affine point of
{\mathcal{Z}}
. Note that
f^{*}
preserves strict functors, i.e., we have a functor

f^{*}:\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{strict}}({\mathcal{Z}})\to\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{strict}}({\mathcal{Y}})

C.3.7.

In particular, for any
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Z}})
, we obtain a functor

f^{!}:{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\operatorname{lax}}\to(f^{*}{\mathbf{C}})^{\operatorname{lax}}_{\mathcal{Y}}

given by

\operatorname{Fun}_{\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Z}})}(\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Z}}),{\mathbf{C}})\to\operatorname{Fun}_{\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})}(f^{*}\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Z}}),f^{*}{\mathbf{C}})\simeq\operatorname{Fun}_{\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})}(\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Y}}),f^{*}{\mathbf{C}}).

Note that
f^{!}
sends
{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\operatorname{strict}}
into
(f^{*}{\mathbf{C}})^{\operatorname{strict}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

When
{\mathbf{C}}=\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathcal{Z}})
, the functor
f^{!}
sends (lax) D-modules of
{\mathcal{Z}}
to (lax) D-modules of
{\mathcal{Y}}
. This construction generalizes the
!
-pullback functors for usual (i.e. non-categorical) prestacks.

C.3.8.
Remark

Following Sect.
C.2.2
, the functor
f^{*}
is given by precomposing with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{Y}}}\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{Z}}}
.

C.3.9.
Example

There is an obvious map
{\operatorname{Ran}}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, which induces a restriction functor

\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})\to\mathbf{CrysCat}({\operatorname{Ran}}).

Here we do not need to distinguish
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\operatorname{Ran}})
and
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{strict}}({\operatorname{Ran}})
because
{\operatorname{Ran}}
is a usual prestack.

C.3.10.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
be categorical prestacks. The
external tensor product
functor

-\boxtimes-:\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})\times\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Z}})\to\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Z}})

is defined to be

{\mathcal{C}}\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}\mathcal{D}:=\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{C}})\otimes\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{D}).

Note that

({\mathcal{C}}\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}\mathcal{D})_{(y,z)}\simeq{\mathcal{C}}_{y}\otimes\mathcal{D}_{z}

for
y:S\to{\mathcal{Y}}
and
z:T\to{\mathcal{Z}}
.

C.3.11.
Warning

For a 2-morphism
f_{1}\to f_{2}:{\mathcal{Y}}\to{\mathcal{Z}}
in
\mathbf{CatPreStk}
, we only have a
left-lax
natural transformation

f_{1}^{*}\to f_{2}^{*}:\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Z}})\to\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}}).

In other words, given a morphism
F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\mathcal{Y}})
, we have a canonical 2-morphism

\textstyle{f_{1}^{*}({\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{2}^{*}({\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{1}^{*}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{2}^{*}({\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}).}

C.4.
Unital factorization categories

C.4.1.

Recall that
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is an abelian monoid object in
\operatorname{CatPreStk}
(see Sect.
C.1.12
).

A
unital factorization category

{\mathbf{A}}
is a crystal of categories
\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
equipped with a
multiplicative structure over the disjoint loci
. In other words, for any finite set
I
, we have an equivalence

(C.2)

\operatorname{mult}_{I}:(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}

and a homotopy-coherent datum of associativity and commutativity. Here
(-)|_{\operatorname{disj}}
means restriction along

\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}}\to\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},

see Sect.
C.1.13
.

C.4.2.
Remark

Roughly speaking, a unital factorization category consists of the following data:

•

DG categories
{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}
and functors
\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}
as in Sect.
C.2.4
;

•

For any finite collection of
disjoint
points in
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, i.e.,

(\underline{x}_{i})_{i\in I}\in\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}},\;|I|<\infty

assign an equivalence

(C.3)

\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}:\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}}\simeq{\mathbf{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}}

(and a datum of associativity and commutatitvity)

such that for two collections
(\underline{x}_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}
of disjoint points satisfying
\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}
, the following diagram commutes
27
27

27

This is also a structure rather than a property, and there is a datum of compatibility with associativity and commutatitvity.

(C.4)

\textstyle{\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\otimes\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ins}_{\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}\subset\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}.}

Note that for
I=\emptyset
, we have a canonical equivalence
\operatorname{mult}_{\emptyset}:\operatorname{Vect}\simeq{\mathbf{A}}_{\emptyset}
28
28

28

Note that for
I=\{1\}
and
\underline{x}_{1}=\emptyset
, we also have an equivalence
\operatorname{mult}_{(\emptyset)}:{\mathbf{A}}_{\emptyset}\simeq{\mathbf{A}}_{\emptyset}
, which is just the identity functor.
.

C.4.3.
Example

The constant crystal
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})
is a unital factorization cateogry by identifying both sides of (
C.2
) with

\underline{\operatorname{D}}\big(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}}\big).

We often denote this factorization category by
\operatorname{Vect}
(because its fiber at any
\underline{x}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
is
\operatorname{Vect}
).

C.4.4.
Example

The factorization structures on
{\operatorname{Gr}}_{G,{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
,
{\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
and
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
upgrade the crystals of categories
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\operatorname{Gr}}_{G})
,
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathfrak{L}}^{+}(G))
,
\underline{\operatorname{D}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))
to unital factorization categories.

C.4.5.
Variant

We define a unital
lax-factorization
cateogry to be a crystal of categories
\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}
over
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
equipped with a
(right-)lax
multiplicative structure over the disjoint loci. This means we replace the equivalence (
C.2
) with a morphism in
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{strict}}(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}})
:

(C.5)

\operatorname{mult}_{I}:(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\to\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}.

One may also consider an even weaker notion, where (
C.5
) is only required to be a morphism in
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}})
. However, we do not see any application of such a structure.

C.4.6.
Remark

The restriction functor
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})\to\mathbf{CrysCat}({\operatorname{Ran}})
sends unital factorization categories to non-unital ones.

C.5.
Morphisms between unital factorization categories

There are (at least) two notions of morphisms between unital factorization categories:
lax-unital functors
and
(strictly) unital functors
.

C.5.1.

Let
{\mathbf{A}}
and
{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
be unital factorization categories. A
lax-unital factorization functor

F:{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
is a morphism
\underline{F}:\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}\to\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{lax}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})
equipped with commutative diagrams (for any finite set
I
)

(C.6)

\textstyle{(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}\underline{F})|_{\operatorname{disj}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{F})|_{\operatorname{disj}}}
\textstyle{(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{\prime})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{\prime})|_{\operatorname{disj}}}

and a homotopy-coherent datum of associativity and commutativity. Here the horizontal arrows are the structural equivalences for
{\mathbf{A}}
and
{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
(see (
C.2
)).

We say
F
is (strictly) unital if
\underline{F}
is contained in
\mathbf{CrysCat}^{\operatorname{strict}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}})
.

C.5.2.

Unital factorization categories and lax-unital factorization functors between them form a 2-category, which is denoted by

\mathbf{UntlFactCat}^{\operatorname{lax-untl}}.

The 1-full subcategory of strictly unital factorization functors is denoted by

\mathbf{UntlFactCat}\subseteq\mathbf{UntlFactCat}^{\operatorname{lax-untl}}.

C.5.3.

Let
{\mathbf{A}}
be a unital factorization category. A
(unital)
29
29

29

When talking about factorization algebras in a
unital
factorization category, we always assume the algebra is unital.
factorization algebra in
{\mathbf{A}}
is
defined
to be a lax-unital factorization functor
{\mathcal{A}}:\operatorname{Vect}\to{\mathbf{A}}
, where
\operatorname{Vect}
is the unital factorization category defined in Sect.
C.4.3
.

Factorization algebras in
{\mathbf{A}}
form a category, which is denoted by
\operatorname{FactAlg}({\mathbf{A}})
.

C.5.4.

Below is a concrete description of factorization algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
in a unital factorization category
{\mathbf{A}}
.

For each finite set
I
, we have a functor

\prod_{i\in I}\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})\to\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})\to\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}\big(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}},(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\big)

that sends

({\mathcal{M}}_{i})_{i\in I}\to(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{M}}_{i})|_{\operatorname{disj}},

and a functor

\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}\big({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},{\mathbf{A}}\big)\to\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}\big(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}},\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}({\mathbf{A}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\big)

that in turn sends

{\mathcal{N}}\mapsto\operatorname{union}_{I}^{!}({\mathcal{N}})|_{\operatorname{disj}},

see Sect.
C.3.7
. Note that the structural equivalence (
C.2
) induces an equivalence

\operatorname{mult}_{I}:\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}\big(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}},(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\big)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}\big(\big(\prod_{i\in I}{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}\big)_{\operatorname{disj}},\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}({\mathbf{A}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\big).

Unwinding the definitions, a factorization algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathbf{A}}
is an object

\underline{{\mathcal{A}}}\in\Gamma^{\operatorname{lax}}({\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}},\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})=:\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}_{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}^{\operatorname{lax}}

equipped with isomorphisms (for any finite set
I
)

(C.7)

\operatorname{mult}_{I}((\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathcal{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{union}_{I}^{!}(\underline{{\mathcal{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}

and a homotopy-coherent datum of associativity and commutativity.

C.5.5.
Remark

Roughly speaking, a factorization algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
in a given unital factorization category
{\mathbf{A}}
consists of the following data:

(i)

Objects
{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}}\in{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}
and morphisms

{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}:\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}\subseteq\underline{x}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}})\to{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}^{\prime}}

as in Sect.
C.3.5
;

(ii)

For any collection
(\underline{x}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
disjoint
points in
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
, an isomorphism

(C.8)

m_{(\underline{x}_{i})}:\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathcal{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}}

that is associative and commutative, where the (invertible) functor
\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}
is as in Sect.
C.4.2
;

such that for two collections
(\underline{x}_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}
of disjoints points satisfying
\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}
, the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\operatorname{ins}_{\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}\subset\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname{ins}_{\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}\subset\sqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})}(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}\operatorname{ins}_{\underline{x}_{i}\subseteq\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime})}(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}^{\prime}},}

where

•

The left vertical isomorphism is due to (
C.4
);

•

The two horizontal isomorphisms are induced by those in (ii);

•

The remaining two arrows are induced by those in (i).

Note that for
I=\emptyset
, we have a canonical isomorphism

m_{\emptyset}:\operatorname{mult}_{\emptyset}(k)\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\mathcal{A}}_{\emptyset},

where
\operatorname{mult}_{\emptyset}
is the canonical identification
\operatorname{Vect}\simeq{\mathbf{A}}_{\emptyset}
.

C.5.6.
Example

For any unital factorization category
{\mathbf{A}}
, there is a unique
unital
factorization functor

{\mathcal{A}}:\operatorname{Vect}\to{\mathbf{A}}.

Namely, unitality implies each
{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}}\in{\mathbf{A}}_{\underline{x}}
is canonically identified with
\operatorname{ins}_{\emptyset\subseteq\underline{x}}({\mathcal{A}}_{\emptyset})
, while
{\mathcal{A}}_{\emptyset}
is canonically identified with
k
via the equivalence
{\mathbf{A}}_{\emptyset}\simeq\operatorname{Vect}
.

We denote this unital factorization functor by

\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}}:\operatorname{Vect}\to{\mathbf{A}}

and view it as an object

\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}}\in\operatorname{FactAlg}({\mathbf{A}}).

The following result is obvious modulo homotopy coherence. A rigorious proof will be provided in
[
CFZ
]
.

Lemma C.5.7
.

Let
{\mathbf{A}}
be a unital factorization category. Then
\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}}
is an initial object in
\operatorname{FactAlg}({\mathbf{A}})
.

C.5.8.

By definition, for any lax unital factorization functor
F:{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
, we have a functor

(C.9)

\operatorname{FactAlg}({\mathbf{A}})\to\operatorname{FactAlg}({\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}),\;{\mathcal{A}}\mapsto F\circ{\mathcal{A}}.

We also write
F({\mathcal{A}}):=F\circ{\mathcal{A}}
.

In particular, we obtain an object
F(\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}})\in\operatorname{FactAlg}({\mathbf{A}}^{\prime})
. By Lemma
C.5.7
, there is a unique morphism

\operatorname{unit}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}}\to F(\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}}).

The following result is obvious modulo homotopy coherence. A rigorious proof will be provided in
[
CFZ
]
.

Lemma C.5.9
.

Let
F:{\mathbf{A}}\to{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
be a lax-unital factorization functor between unital factorization categories. Then
F
is strictly unital iff the canonical morphism
\operatorname{unit}_{{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}}\to F(\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}})
is invertible.

C.5.10.
Variant

The notion of (lax-)unital factorization functors makes sense also for unital lax-factorization categories (see Sect.
C.4.5
). In particular, we can define the object
\operatorname{unit}_{\mathbf{A}}
for any unital lax-factorization cateogry
{\mathbf{A}}
30
30

30

Warning: Lemma
C.5.9
needs
{\mathbf{A}}
to be a unital (strict-)factorization category, although
{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
can be lax.
.

One can consider an even weaker notion of factorization functors, where the commutative diagram (
C.6
) is replaced with a 2-morphism

\textstyle{(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}\underline{F})|_{\operatorname{disj}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{{\mathbf{A}}})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{F})|_{\operatorname{disj}}}
\textstyle{(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits_{i\in I}}\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{\prime})|_{\operatorname{disj}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{union}_{I}^{*}(\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{\prime})|_{\operatorname{disj}}.}

A morphism
\underline{F}:\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}\to\underline{{\mathbf{A}}}^{\prime}
equipped with such a structure is called a
lax-unital lax-factorization functor
. Taking
{\mathbf{A}}:=\operatorname{Vect}
, we obtain the notion of
lax-factorization algebras
in a unital lax-factorization category
{\mathbf{A}}^{\prime}
. One can describe these objects as in Sect.
C.5.5
by replacing the isomorphism
m_{(\underline{x}_{i})_{i\in I}}
with a morphism

m_{(\underline{x}_{i})}:\operatorname{mult}_{(\underline{x}_{i})}(\mathbin{\mathop{\boxtimes}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{x}_{i}})\to{\mathcal{A}}_{\bigsqcup\underline{x}_{i}}.

C.6.
Unital factorization module categories

C.6.1.

Let
\underline{x}_{0}\in{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}(S_{0})
be an affine point. The
\underline{x}_{0}
-marked unital Ran space
is the category prestack
{\operatorname{Ran}}_{\underline{x}_{0}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
over
S_{0}
that attaches to an affine test
S_{0}
-scheme
S
the category of finite subsets
\underline{y}\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}(S,X)
that contain the image of
\underline{x}_{0}
under the restriction map

\operatorname{Hom}(S_{0},X)\to\operatorname{Hom}(S,X).

Note that there is a canonical morphism
S_{0}\to{\operatorname{Ran}}_{\underline{x}_{0}}
corresponding to
\underline{x}_{0}\subseteq\operatorname{Hom}(S_{0},X)
.

Also note that
{\operatorname{Ran}}_{\emptyset}^{\operatorname{untl}}={\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
.

C.6.2.
Remark

Let
\widetilde{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}\to(\operatorname{Sch}^{\operatorname{aff}})^{\operatorname{op}}
be the coCartesian fibration corresponding to
{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}
(see Sect.
C.2.2
). We can view
\underline{x}_{0}
as an object
\widetilde{{\operatorname{Ran}}^{\operatorname{untl}}}
lying over
S_{0}
. By definition,
